        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\label{sec:why_kstar}\n\\label{sec:one_wash}\n\\label{sec:negative_graduated}\n\\label{sec:repr_dependence}\n\\label{sec:boundary_cases}\n\\label{sec:length_confound}\n\n\\paragraph{One-wash laundering (web text).}\nFor general web text, $\\Kempirical{=}2$ reflects concentrated information loss: 0v1 is near-trivially separable ($\\auc \\geq 0.92$), 1v2 drops by ${\\sim}$30\\,pp, and 2v3 falls below $\\theta$.\nA single generation pass renders text indistinguishable from multiply recycled content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.\nConverging evidence supports a data-intrinsic interpretation: OBD achieves 96\\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 8--38\\% (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\\ OBD; Table~\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\n\n\\paragraph{Graduated filtering failure.}\nFor web text, 2v3 $\\auc < \\theta$ across all model configurations, so depth assignments for $d \\geq 2$ approximate random labels; graduated weighting injects noise, explaining convergence toward binary at high $\\alpha$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\nDSIR fails differently: bigram hashing captures domain shift but not depth-correlated signatures.\n\n\\paragraph{Representation dependence.}\n$\\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent: frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$, while Pythia yields $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$ and OLMo yields $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 1$, consistent with its 15D boundary (Table~\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}).\nThe 15D features trade one depth level of sensitivity for cross-model consistency (6/10 configurations $\\Kempirical{=}2$), making them the preferred operational choice.\nFeature rankings are scorer-independent ($\\rho > 0.90$); $\\Kempirical$ agreement is scorer-independent for strong-signal configurations (e.g., Qwen-rewrite retains $\\Kempirical{=}2$ under all scorers) but scorer-sensitive at marginal boundaries (Qwen-continuation drops to $\\Kempirical{=}1$; length confound ruled out in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\nANOVA confirms this layered pattern: Scorer explains modest variance ($\\eta^2_p{=}.166$) with a significant Generator$\\times$Scorer interaction ($\\eta^2_p{=}.146$; Table~\\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}).\n\n\\paragraph{Domain dependence of $\\Kempirical$.}\nWeb text (C4, Wikipedia) consistently yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$, while structured academic text (arXiv) yields $\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent model families (Qwen-1.5B, Pythia-1.4B; Section~\\ref{sec:cross_domain}).\nDomain-specific stylistic constraints amplify per-generation distributional shift, making contamination in structured corpora trackable to greater depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-chain scope and retrain-chain gap.}\nOur prompt-chain protocol targets the dominant web contamination scenario: LLM-generated text is published, scraped into training corpora, and recursively re-generated by downstream models without retraining on the outputs.\nRetrain-chain experiments (Pythia-1.4B LoRA) yield $\\Kempirical{\\geq}4$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.9484$, 3v4 $= 0.7982$), far exceeding the prompt-chain boundary of $\\Kempirical{=}2$, because each retrain generation also modifies model parameters, introducing cumulative adaptation artifacts (vocabulary narrowing, repetition bias) absent from text-only recycling.\nPrompt-chain $\\Kempirical$ therefore isolates text-intrinsic degradation, the operationally relevant quantity for data curation pipelines that filter documents without knowledge of the generator's training history.\nOur conclusions do not directly transfer to retrain-chain (model collapse) settings, where the higher $\\Kempirical$ reflects model-level rather than text-level information loss.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary cases and scale effect.}\n$\\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations cluster at 1v2 $\\auc$ 0.508--0.598, well separated from the $\\Kempirical{=}2$ range (0.611--0.696); $\\Kempirical{=}1$ is not a classifier failure but a text property, as these models produce first-generation output so human-like that statistical features cannot distinguish depth~1 from depth~2.\nThat $\\Kempirical \\in \\{1, 2\\}$ for all web text configurations is the core empirical result; larger models reduce 0v1 JSD (0.077 at 8B vs.\\ 0.15--0.27 at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting 13B+ may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategy as confound.}\nGreedy decoding inflates depth separability: Pythia-6.9B yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ under greedy but $\\Kempirical{=}1$ under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$), with the borderline 1v2 score (2 of 5 folds above $\\theta$) illustrating that the boundary is continuous rather than a cliff (Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_decoding}).\nPractitioners should calibrate $\\theta$ to their application-specific tolerance (Figure~\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).\n\n\\paragraph{What $\\Kempirical$ reveals.}\nAs a measurement tool, $\\Kempirical$ provides explanatory power beyond a point estimate: it establishes why binary detection suffices for web text (depth${>}1$ is invisible to statistical features), identifies domains where finer strategies are viable (arXiv $\\Kempirical{=}3$ vs.\\ web $\\Kempirical{=}2$), and quantifies how scale, scorer, and threshold jointly shape the boundary.\nThe OBD framework provides a reproducible, low-cost depth estimation protocol requiring only corpus-level statistical features, unlike detection methods that need API access or model weights.\nFuture work may explore scorer-ensemble strategies to improve cross-scorer robustness at marginal boundaries.\n",
        "numLines": 47,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 47
      }

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/main.tex",
        "content": "% EMNLP 2026 Main — ARR May 2026\n% Generational Depth Contamination\n\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\\usepackage[section]{placeins}\n\n\\newcommand{\\Kstar}{{K^*}}\n\\newcommand{\\Kempirical}{{K^*_{\\text{empirical}}}}\n\\newcommand{\\Ktheory}{{K^*_{\\text{theory}}}}\n\\newcommand{\\auc}{\\text{AUC}}\n\\newcommand{\\jsd}{\\text{JSD}}\n\n\\title{Generational Depth Estimation of Recursive Synthetic Text:\\\\Measuring the Discrimination Boundary}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\begin{abstract}\nWeb-scale corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational discrimination boundary, $\\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain distinguishable), using 15 statistical features and an OBD-LightGBM classifier.\nUnder prompt-chain generation across 10 model configurations (6 architecture families, 1B--8B), 3 source domains, and 4 decoding strategies, $\\Kempirical \\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six families, 1B--8B) at $\\theta{=}0.60$; for web text (C4, Wikipedia), $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, with structured academic text (arXiv) reaching $\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two architectures.\nAmong the six nominal $\\Kempirical{=}2$ configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\nA single generation pass suffices to render general web text indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content, a \\emph{one-wash laundering} effect.\nModel-free statistical features enable 80--100\\% cross-model transfer with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\\rho > 0.90$).\nAs a consistency check, domain-appropriate $\\Kempirical$ correctly predicts that binary filtering outperforms graduated alternatives for web text when contamination causes detectable quality loss.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n% === Section 1: Introduction ===\n\\input{introduction}\n\n% === Section 2: Related Work ===\n\\input{related_work}\n\n% === Section 3: Method ===\n\\input{method}\n\n% === Section 4: Experiments ===\n\\input{experiments_c1}\n\n% === Section 5: K*-Aware Data Filtering ===\n\\input{experiments_c3}\n\n% === Section 6: Discussion ===\n\\input{discussion}\n\n% === Section 7: Conclusion ===\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n% === Limitations (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur findings are bounded by:\n(1)~\\emph{Scope}: model scale $\\leq$8B (6 architecture families), English text (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv), four decoding strategies; arXiv $\\Kempirical{=}3$ is confirmed with two model families (Qwen-1.5B, Pythia-1.4B) but with reduced sample size ($n{=}2{,}200$ per model).\n(2)~\\emph{Protocol}: prompt-chain rather than retrain-chain. Retrain-chain validation (Pythia-1.4B LoRA, Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}) yields $\\Kempirical{\\geq}4$ due to cumulative model-adaptation artifacts; prompt-chain isolates text-intrinsic degradation and provides the operationally relevant boundary for data curation.\n(3)~\\emph{Threshold}: $\\Kempirical$ ranges from 2--3 at $\\theta{=}0.55$ to 1 at $\\theta{=}0.70$; 6/10 configurations yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (OLMo-1B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-2B, Pythia-6.9B: $\\Kempirical{=}1$).\n(4)~\\emph{Marginal boundary}: Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation drops this to $\\Kempirical{=}1$ (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}).\n(5)~\\emph{Feature set}: the 15D features yield a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher); neural probe $\\Kempirical$ is model-dependent ($3$ for Qwen, $2$ for Pythia, $1$ for OLMo).\n(6)~\\emph{Segment length}: $\\Kempirical{=}2$ confirmed for 128 and 256 tokens (CI lower $> \\theta$); 512 tokens yields point estimate $\\Kempirical{=}2$ but CI lower marginally below $\\theta$ ($0.5979$), indicating the boundary weakens at longer generation lengths.\nLonger documents may require larger sample sizes or more sensitive feature sets to maintain the boundary; whether this reflects genuine information-theoretic limits or finite-sample attenuation remains open.\n(7)~\\emph{Curation applicability}: the practical benefit of $\\Kstar$-aware curation depends on contamination severity; when synthetic data proportion is low enough to leave overall training quality intact, filtering strategies converge (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\nOur filtering validation (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}) was conducted exclusively on Qwen-1.5B continuation-mode text; extending to $\\Kempirical{=}1$ generators remains future work.\nSeed robustness testing (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_robustness}) shows $\\Kempirical$ is stable under seed variation for strong-signal configurations, but marginal boundaries are seed-sensitive (absolute AUC shifts up to ${\\pm}9$\\,pp).\nAdditional limitations (cross-model scoring sensitivity, feature ranking variability at the 1v2 boundary, structural composition confound analysis) appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.\n\n% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nGenerational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing, positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.\nOur experiments use the publicly available C4 corpus and do not involve personally identifiable information.\nA potential dual-use concern is that depth estimation techniques could inform adversarial strategies to evade detection (e.g., by calibrating rewriting to mimic earlier-generation statistics); we note that this risk is shared by all detection research and that the binary detection boundary ($\\auc \\geq 0.96$ at 0v1) remains robust.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_extended}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\n$\\Kempirical \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$ depends on source domain and model characteristics: general web text yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ in 6/10 configurations (three of six families contain $\\Kempirical{\\geq}2$ configurations, with a scale-dependent split in Pythia; 1B--8B; $p \\leq 0.002$), with $\\Kempirical{=}3$ for structured academic text.\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.\nFor practitioners, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\ $\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal; graduated weighting beyond $\\Kempirical$ adds noise.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent (15D provides a conservative lower bound; neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures) and established for 1B--8B models; scaling beyond 8B, additional structured domains, and retrain-chain $\\Kstar$ remain open.\n",

_source line 18217 · 2026-05-22T10:21:50.721Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017oZ5AK385mDTXJP8NW2pCH`</summary>

```text
1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
2	"""Pairwise AUC decay curves: auto-discovers experiment JSONs and plots all configs.
3	
4	Input:  results_*/pairwise_auc.json, results/*/pairwise_auc.json, data_*/pairwise_auc.json
5	Output: artifacts/figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf
6	Deps:   matplotlib, numpy
7	"""
8	
9	import argparse
10	import json
11	import re
12	import matplotlib
13	matplotlib.use("Agg")
14	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
15	import numpy as np
16	from pathlib import Path
17	
18	# ── Color/style rotation ──
19	TABLEAU_COLORS = [
20	    "#4e79a7", "#e15759", "#76b7b2", "#59a14f",
21	    "#b07aa1", "#ff9da7", "#9c755f", "#bab0ac",
22	    "#edc948", "#f28e2b",
23	]
24	MARKERS = ["o", "s", "^", "D", "v", "P", "X", "*", "h", "<"]
25	LINESTYLES_SOLID = ["-", "-", "-", "-", "-", "-", "-", "-"]
26	LINESTYLES_DASHED = ["--", "--", "--", "--", "--", "--", "--", "--"]
27	
28	
29	def infer_label(dir_path: Path) -> tuple[str, str]:
30	    """Infer display label and group (model/decode) from directory name."""
31	    name = dir_path.name
32	
33	    decode_patterns = {
34	        "greedy": "Greedy", "nuc09": "Nuc p=0.9", "nuc0.9": "Nuc p=0.9",
35	        "t07": "T=0.7", "t0.7": "T=0.7", "t12": "T=1.2", "t1.2": "T=1.2",
36	    }
37	    for pat, label in decode_patterns.items():
38	        if pat in name.lower():
39	            return label, "decode"
40	
41	    model_patterns = [
42	        (r"qwen.*rw|qwen.*rewrite", "Qwen-rewrite"),
43	        (r"qwen.*base", "Qwen-base"),
44	        (r"qwen.*7b", "Qwen-7B"),
45	        (r"qwen.*cont|qwen(?!.*rw|.*rewrite|.*base|.*7b)", "Qwen-cont"),
46	        (r"pythia", "Pythia-cont"),
47	        (r"olmo", "OLMo-cont"),
48	        (r"mistral.*7b", "Mistral-7B"),
49	        (r"llama.*8b", "LLaMA-8B"),
50	    ]
51	    for pat, label in model_patterns:
52	        if re.search(pat, name, re.IGNORECASE):
53	            return label, "model"
54	
55	    clean = name.replace("results_", "").replace("results", "").replace("data_", "")
56	    clean = re.sub(r"^exp\d+_?", "", clean).strip("_") or name
57	    return clean, "model"
58	
59	
60	def discover_auc_files(root: Path) -> list[Path]:
61	    """Find all pairwise_auc.json under results_*/, results/*/, data_*/."""
62	    patterns = [
63	        "results_*/pairwise_auc.json",
64	        "results/*/pairwise_auc.json",
65	        "data_*/pairwise_auc.json",
66	    ]
67	    files = []
68	    for pat in patterns:
69	        files.extend(sorted(root.glob(pat)))
70	    return files
71	
72	
73	def load_auc_data(json_path: Path) -> dict[str, float] | None:
74	    """Load pairwise AUC from JSON. Returns {pair: auc} dict or None."""
75	    with open(json_path) as f:
76	        raw = json.load(f)
77	    if "mean" in raw:
78	        return raw["mean"]
79	    pairs = {}
80	    for key in ["0v1", "1v2", "2v3", "3v4", "4v5"]:
81	        if key in raw:
82	            val = raw[key]
83	            if isinstance(val, dict):
84	                pairs[key] = val.get("auc", val.get("mean"))
85	            else:
86	                pairs[key] = val
87	    return pairs if pairs else None
88	
89	
90	def parse_args():
91	    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Plot pairwise AUC decay curves")
92	    parser.add_argument("--results-dir", type=Path,
93	                        default=Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent.parent,
94	                        help="Root directory to scan for results")
95	    parser.add_argument("--output", type=Path, default=None,
96	                        help="Output PDF path (default: artifacts/figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf)")
97	    parser.add_argument("--filter", type=str, default=None,
98	                        help="Filter models by substring (case-insensitive)")
99	    return parser.parse_args()
100	
101	
102	def main():
103	    args = parse_args()
104	    root = args.results_dir
105	
106	    auc_files = discover_auc_files(root)
107	    if not auc_files:
108	        print(f"No pairwise_auc.json found under {root}")
109	        return
110	
111	    models = {}  # label -> {group, data}
112	    for fp in auc_files:
113	        parent = fp.parent
114	        label, group = infer_label(parent)
115	        data = load_auc_data(fp)
116	        if data is None:
117	            continue
118	        if args.filter and args.filter.lower() not in label.lower():
119	            continue
120	        models[label] = {"group": group, "data": data}
121	
122	    if not models:
123	        print("No valid AUC data found after filtering")
124	        return
125	
126	    model_items = [(k, v) for k, v in models.items() if v["group"] == "model"]
127	    decode_items = [(k, v) for k, v in models.items() if v["group"] == "decode"]
128	
129	    x_labels = ["0 vs 1", "1 vs 2", "2 vs 3", "3 vs 4", "4 vs 5"]
130	    pair_keys = ["0v1", "1v2", "2v3", "3v4", "4v5"]
131	    x = np.arange(len(x_labels))
132	
133	    # ── Style ──
134	    plt.rcParams.update({
135	        "font.family": "serif",
136	        "font.size": 11,
137	        "axes.linewidth": 0.8,
138	        "xtick.major.width": 0.8,
139	        "ytick.major.width": 0.8,
140	    })
141	
142	    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), constrained_layout=True)
143	
144	    ax.axvspan(0.5, 1.5, color="#fff9c4", alpha=0.55, zorder=0)
145	    ax.text(1.0, 1.02, r"$K^*$ boundary", ha="center", va="bottom", fontsize=9,
146	            fontstyle="italic", color="#8d6e00")
147	
148	    ax.axhline(0.60, color="grey", ls="--", lw=0.8, zorder=1)

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate generational depth estimation on ten model configurations spanning six architecture families:
5	Pythia-1.4B, Pythia-6.9B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base), Qwen2.5-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and Gemma-2-2B, all in continuation mode except where noted.
6	All experiments use the OBD-LightGBM classifier with the 15-dimensional feature set (Section~\ref{sec:method}), with 5{,}000 texts per depth level (generations 0--5).
7	
8	%% ====================================================================
9	\subsection{$\Kempirical \leq 2$ Across Model Configurations (C4)}
10	\label{sec:kstar_boundary}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]

16	$\Kempirical = 2$ requires 1v2 $\auc > \theta{=}0.60$ and 2v3 $\auc < \theta$; CIs quantify boundary uncertainty.
17	Extended results (all models) in Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}.}

22	\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\cmidrule(lr){10-11}

25	0v1 & .9956 & & .9932 & & .9860 & & .9971 & & .9638 & [.961,\,.967] \\

27	2v3 & .5893 & [.580,\,.599] & .5514 & [.542,\,.563] & .4973 & [.486,\,.509] & .5609 & [.551,\,.571] & .5492 & [.539,\,.559] \\
28	3v4 & .5435 & & .5152 & & .5024\rlap{$^\S$} & & .5409 & & .5276 & \\

30	\bottomrule

32	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^\ddagger$Generator: Qwen2.5-7B (fp16); scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B.} \\
33	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^\S$OLMo 2v3 (0.497) $<$ 3v4 (0.502): non-monotonic reversal within noise ($\Delta = 0.005$).} \\
34	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize Full results for all 10 configurations (including Qwen-7B, Qwen-base, Gemma-2-2B, and Pythia-6.9B) are provided in Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}.}

38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	\centering
40	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf}
41	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ decay across depth pairs for model-mode configurations (solid) and decoding strategies (dashed); shaded bands show bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals. The horizontal dashed line marks $\theta = 0.60$.}
42	\label{fig:auc_decay}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci} and Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} show $\Kempirical \leq 2$ across all 10 model configurations: 6/10 configurations yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Qwen with 4 configs, LLaMA-8B, and Pythia-1.4B, spanning three of six families) (1v2 $\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955; all CI lower bounds $> \theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), while OLMo-1B, Mistral-7B, Pythia-6.9B, and Gemma-2-2B yield $\Kempirical{=}1$ (1v2 $\auc$: 0.508, 0.594, 0.596, and 0.598, all below $\theta$; Section~\ref{sec:boundary_cases}).
46	Under Bonferroni correction for 10 simultaneous comparisons (99.5\% CIs), four of six boundary configurations retain CI lower bounds above $\theta$; the two most marginal cases, Qwen-continuation (nominal CI lower bound 0.602) and LLaMA-8B (0.603), fall below the widened interval.
47	Both retain point estimates above $\theta$ and uncorrected CI lower bounds above $\theta$; the near-misses reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.
48	By architecture family: Qwen (4/4) and LLaMA (1/1) yield $\Kempirical{\geq}2$ at nominal significance; OLMo (1/1), Mistral (1/1), and Gemma (1/1) yield $\Kempirical{=}1$; Pythia exhibits a scale-dependent split (1.4B: $\Kempirical{=}2$, 6.9B: $\Kempirical{=}1$).
49	LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms $\Kempirical{=}2$ (0v1 $\auc = 0.9225$, 1v2 $\auc = 0.6123\;[0.603,\,0.622]$; CI lower bound above $\theta$; Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}).
50	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
51	The 1v2 spread due to generation mode ($\Delta = 8.4$\,pp within Qwen) exceeds the spread across $\Kempirical{=}2$ continuation models ($\Delta = 4.3$\,pp).
52	
53	\paragraph{Scale and instruction-tuning invariance.}
54	$\Kempirical{=}2$ persists from 1.5B through 8B: Qwen-7B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6123$) both exceed $\theta$, despite progressively reduced 0v1 signal (0.9638 and 0.9225 vs.\ $> 0.99$ at $\leq$1.5B).
55	A base-vs.-instruct ablation at 1.5B likewise yields $\Kempirical = 2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.626$), confirming the boundary is not an instruction-tuning artifact.
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_kstar_theta.pdf}
60	\caption{$\Kempirical(\theta)$ sensitivity curve for six configurations. $\Kempirical$: maximum discriminable depth; $\theta$: AUC decision threshold. Five of six configurations yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (dashed line); OLMo yields $\Kempirical{=}1$. At $\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\Kempirical{=}1$.}

decode_handles.append(line)\n\nax.set_xticks(x)\nax.set_xticklabels(x_labels)\nax.set_xlabel(\"Depth Pair\", fontsize=8)\nax.set_ylabel(\"Pairwise AUC (5-fold CV)\", fontsize=8)\nax.set_ylim(0.45, However, I notice two issues:
1. The K* boundary yellow band is too wide - it covers from 0v1 to 1v2 area, but in the original it was narrower (just around the 1v2 region). Actually, looking at the original, it was `axvspan(0.5, 1.5)` which spans from between 0v1 and 1v2 to between 1v2 and 2v3. 

The span is actually the same in my script - it just looks wider because the figure is more compact now, so I'll leave it as is.

2. The "T=1.2" line uses the same teal/cyan color as OLMo, making them hard to distinguish. I need to adjust the decode colors to avoid this collision with the model colors.

Looking at the original figure, T=1.2 was also teal but dashed, which differentiated it from the solid OLMo line. The original script uses the TABLEAU color rotation starting after the models, so I'm checking how the indices map out - models take the first seven colors, then decoding uses the remaining ones with wrapping.

I see the issue now: my custom decode colors include #76b7b2 for T=1.2, which is the same as OLMo's color from the TABLEAU palette. That's a collision I need to fix by picking a different color for that temperature. I'm realizing the original figure actually uses a dashed line for T=1.2 to distinguish it from the solid teal OLMo line, so the color collision wasn't really a problem. Since the Director didn't flag this as an issue, maybe I'm overthinking the color choice and should just stick with the original approach of wrapping through TABLEAU with a dashed line distinction. I'm mapping out the color indices from the original script—the decode items follow a TABLEAU rotation starting at index 7, which gives me grey, gold, orange, and blue when wrapping around. But the visual order doesn't quite match what the auto-discovery would produce, so I'm focusing on whether my generated figure actually has the color assignments right and if the x-axis label overlap is genuinely a problem worth fixing. I've already addressed the x-axis label overlap issue, so I'll skip the color changes and move forward. Let me compile the paper now to verify that both the table and figure fixes are working correctly.

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate generational depth estimation on ten model configurations spanning six architecture families:
5	Pythia-1.4B, Pythia-6.9B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base), Qwen2.5-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and Gemma-2-2B, all in continuation mode except where noted.
6	All experiments use the OBD-LightGBM classifier with the 15-dimensional feature set (Section~\ref{sec:method}), with 5{,}000 texts per depth level (generations 0--5).
7	
8	%% ====================================================================
9	\subsection{$\Kempirical \leq 2$ Across Model Configurations (C4)}
10	\label{sec:kstar_boundary}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]
13	\centering
14	\small
15	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ for adjacent depth pairs with bootstrap 95\% CIs and permutation $p$-values for boundary rows.
16	$\Kempirical = 2$ requires 1v2 $\auc > \theta{=}0.60$ and 2v3 $\auc < \theta$; CIs quantify boundary uncertainty.
17	Extended results (all models) in Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}.}
18	\label{tab:kstar_ci}
19	\begin{tabular}{lcccccccccc}
20	\toprule
21	 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-rw} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Pythia} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{OLMo} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-cont} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-7B\rlap{$^\ddagger$}} \\
22	\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\cmidrule(lr){10-11}
23	 & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI \\
24	\midrule
25	0v1 & .9956 & & .9932 & & .9860 & & .9971 & & .9638 & [.961,\,.967] \\

27	2v3 & .5893 & [.580,\,.599] & .5514 & [.542,\,.563] & .4973 & [.486,\,.509] & .5609 & [.551,\,.571] & .5492 & [.539,\,.559] \\
28	3v4 & .5435 & & .5152 & & .5024\rlap{$^\S$} & & .5409 & & .5276 & \\
29	4v5 & .5340 & & .5136 & & .4942 & & .5141 & & .5085 & \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^*$Permutation $p < 0.001$ for all 1v2 comparisons shown.} \\
32	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^\ddagger$Generator: Qwen2.5-7B (fp16); scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B.} \\
33	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^\S$OLMo 2v3 (0.497) $<$ 3v4 (0.502): non-monotonic reversal within noise ($\Delta = 0.005$).} \\
34	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize Full results for all 10 configurations (including Qwen-7B, Qwen-base, Gemma-2-2B, and Pythia-6.9B) are provided in Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}.}
35	\end{tabular}
36	\end{table*}
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	\centering
40	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf}
41	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ decay across depth pairs for model-mode configurations (solid) and decoding strategies (dashed); shaded bands show bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals. The horizontal dashed line marks $\theta = 0.60$.}
42	\label{fig:auc_decay}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci} and Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} show $\Kempirical \leq 2$ across all 10 model configurations: 6/10 configurations yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Qwen with 4 configs, LLaMA-8B, and Pythia-1.4B, spanning three of six families) (1v2 $\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955; all CI lower bounds $> \theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), while OLMo-1B, Mistral-7B, Pythia-6.9B, and Gemma-2-2B yield $\Kempirical{=}1$ (1v2 $\auc$: 0.508, 0.594, 0.596, and 0.598, all below $\theta$; Section~\ref{sec:boundary_cases}).
46	Under Bonferroni correction for 10 simultaneous comparisons (99.5\% CIs), four of six boundary configurations retain CI lower bounds above $\theta$; the two most marginal cases, Qwen-continuation (nominal CI lower bound 0.602) and LLaMA-8B (0.603), fall below the widened interval.
47	Both retain point estimates above $\theta$ and uncorrected CI lower bounds above $\theta$; the near-misses reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.
48	By architecture family: Qwen (4/4) and LLaMA (1/1) yield $\Kempirical{\geq}2$ at nominal significance; OLMo (1/1), Mistral (1/1), and Gemma (1/1) yield $\Kempirical{=}1$; Pythia exhibits a scale-dependent split (1.4B: $\Kempirical{=}2$, 6.9B: $\Kempirical{=}1$).
49	LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms $\Kempirical{=}2$ (0v1 $\auc = 0.9225$, 1v2 $\auc = 0.6123\;[0.603,\,0.622]$; CI lower bound above $\theta$; Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}).
50	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
51	The 1v2 spread due to generation mode ($\Delta = 8.4$\,pp within Qwen) exceeds the spread across $\Kempirical{=}2$ continuation models ($\Delta = 4.3$\,pp).
52	
53	\paragraph{Scale and instruction-tuning invariance.}
54	$\Kempirical{=}2$ persists from 1.5B through 8B: Qwen-7B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (1v2 $\auc = 0.6123$) both exceed $\theta$, despite progressively reduced 0v1 signal (0.9638 and 0.9225 vs.\ $> 0.99$ at $\leq$1.5B).
55	A base-vs.-instruct ablation at 1.5B likewise yields $\Kempirical = 2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.626$), confirming the boundary is not an instruction-tuning artifact.
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_kstar_theta.pdf}
60	\caption{$\Kempirical(\theta)$ sensitivity curve for six configurations. $\Kempirical$: maximum discriminable depth; $\theta$: AUC decision threshold. Five of six configurations yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (dashed line); OLMo yields $\Kempirical{=}1$. At $\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\Kempirical{=}1$.}
61	\label{fig:kstar_theta}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	The raw AUC gap between adjacent generations provides a threshold-free continuous measure of discriminability; Figure~\ref{fig:kstar_theta} displays the full sensitivity surface across $\theta \in [0.55, 0.70]$, allowing practitioners to read off $\Kempirical$ at any desired effect-size threshold.
65	Five of six configurations yield $\Kempirical = 2$ within $\theta \in [0.51, 0.61]$ (OLMo: $\Kempirical = 1$ throughout); at $\theta{=}0.55$, two Qwen configurations reach $\Kempirical = 3$; at $\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\Kempirical = 1$.
66	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical = 2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (1v2 $\auc \in [0.687, 0.704]$; Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
67	
68	%% ====================================================================
69	\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}
70	\label{sec:transfer}
71	
72	\begin{figure}[t]
73	\centering
74	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}
75	\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap. Each cell shows 6-class ordinal accuracy when trained on the row model and tested on the column model, divided by in-domain accuracy (retention rate). Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 90--92\%; Qwen-rewrite$\to$continuation: 61--63\%.}
76	\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}
77	\end{figure}
78	
79	Continuation-mode classifiers transfer well across generators (Figure~\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap}; full matrix in Table~\ref{tab:s1_transfer}): Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\%, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\%, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\% (0v1 cross-model $\auc > 0.96$ for all pairs).
80	Qwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\%), confirming reliance on mode-specific artifacts rather than generalizable depth features.
81	
82	\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation.}
83	Replacing the self-scorer with Pythia-1.4B or OLMo-1B preserves feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$) but attenuates absolute discriminability at the boundary (Table~\ref{tab:crossscorer}).
84	Feature ranking independence is a strong result: the same features dominate under all scorers.
85	$\Kempirical$ agreement, however, is signal-strength dependent: Qwen-rewrite (strong signal, 1v2 $\auc = 0.696$) retains $\Kempirical{=}2$ under all scorers, but Qwen-continuation (marginal, 1v2 $\auc = 0.611$, only 0.011 above $\theta$) drops to $\Kempirical{=}1$ (Pythia: $0.5923$; OLMo: $0.5858$).
86	A three-way ANOVA (Generator $\times$ Scorer $\times$ Depth-Pair; Table~\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}) confirms a statistically significant but modest Scorer main effect ($\eta^2_p{=}.166$, $p < .001$) and a significant Generator$\times$Scorer interaction ($\eta^2_p{=}.146$), indicating that absolute AUC values are scorer-dependent even when $\Kempirical$ classifications largely agree.
87	
88	%% ====================================================================
89	\subsection{Feature Ordering and Generation Mode}
90	\label{sec:feature_degradation}
91	\label{sec:generation_mode}
92	
93	Among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\rho \geq 0.85$; Table~\ref{tab:top_features}).
94	The top-3 features (\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std}, \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}) are shared across all three families, revealing that autoregressive generation progressively damps surprisal variability.
95	Qwen-rewrite diverges (\texttt{freq\_entropy} dominates), consistent with its poor cross-model transfer (61--63\%).
96	Within Qwen, rewrite yields 1v2 $\auc = 0.6955$ vs.\ continuation's $0.6112$ ($\Delta = +8.4$\,pp); continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition.
97	
98	%% ====================================================================
99	\subsection{Neural Probe}
100	\label{sec:neural_probe}
101	
102	Replacing the 15D features with frozen encoder hidden states (MLP probe; Table~\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}), Qwen achieves $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.6159$), while Pythia remains at $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 2$ ($\auc_{2v3} = 0.5963$); OLMo yields $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 1$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.5054$, consistent with its 15D boundary).
103	We hypothesize that the Qwen-only elevation reflects self-familiarity artifacts, consistent with the poor transfer observed in Section~\ref{sec:transfer}; the 15D features yield $\Kempirical = 2$ for most families (OLMo: $\Kempirical = 1$).
104	
105	\noindent\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
106	\centering
107	\footnotesize
108	\captionof{table}{Neural probe pairwise AUC using frozen-encoder MLP. Each cell reports AUC for classifying depth $d$ vs.\ $d{+}1$ text. $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}$: maximum depth where AUC exceeds $\theta = 0.60$.}
109	\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}
110	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
111	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
112	\toprule
113	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{2v3 $\auc$} & \textbf{95\% CI} & $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}$ \\
114	\midrule
115	Qwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\,0.622]$ & 3 \\
116	Pythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\,0.603]$ & 2 \\
117	OLMo-1B    & 0.5056 & $[0.497,\,0.516]$ & 1\rlap{$^\dagger$} \\
118	\bottomrule
119	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$OLMo 1v2 $\auc = 0.5054$ (below $\theta$); $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 1$.}
120	\end{tabular}}
121	\end{minipage}
122	
123	%% ====================================================================
124	\subsection{Cross-Domain Variation}
125	\label{sec:cross_domain}
126	
127	The preceding experiments use C4 (general web text).
128	To test domain dependence, we evaluate on Wikipedia ($n{=}5{,}000$) and arXiv abstracts ($n{=}2{,}200$) using two model families.
129	Wikipedia yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.623$, 2v3 $\auc = 0.571$), matching the C4 baseline.
130	Both Qwen-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B yield $\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv (Table~\ref{tab:domain_kstar}): 2v3 $\auc = 0.644\;[0.628,\,0.658]$ and $0.655\;[0.640,\,0.669]$ respectively (bootstrap CI lower bounds above $\theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), demonstrating that structured academic text permits discrimination to greater depth independently of architecture.
131	The elevated signal reflects higher per-generation distributional shift (1v2 JSD $= 0.077$ vs.\ ${\sim}0.01$ for C4), as formulaic structure amplifies deviation from domain norms.
132	
133	\begin{table}[t]
134	\centering
135	\footnotesize
136	\caption{Cross-domain $\Kempirical$ comparison. Rows: generator model and text domain (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv). $\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv indicates structured text preserves discriminability to greater depth.}
137	\label{tab:domain_kstar}
138	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
139	\toprule
140	\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{1v2 $\auc$} & \textbf{2v3 $\auc$} & $\Kempirical$ \\
141	\midrule
142	arXiv & Qwen-1.5B  & 0.858 & 0.6436 & 3 \\
143	arXiv & Pythia-1.4B & 0.805 & 0.6545 & 3 \\
144	C4    & Qwen-1.5B  & 0.63  & 0.56   & 2 \\
145	Wikipedia & Qwen-1.5B & 0.62 & 0.55 & 2 \\
146	\bottomrule
147	\end{tabular}
148	\end{table}
149	
150	%% ====================================================================
151	\subsection{Binary Detector Confidence}
152	\label{sec:binary_confidence}
153	
154	Binary detectors (DivEye~\cite{basani2026diveye}, Binoculars~\cite{hans2024binoculars}) show moderate rank correlations with depth ($|\rho| = 0.06$--$0.46$; Table~\ref{tab:s9_binary_spearman}) but fail as depth proxies: 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\leq 0.199$ vs.\ 0.167 baseline), pairwise $\auc$ yields $\Kempirical^{\text{binary}} \leq 1$, and multiple conditions exhibit non-monotonic AUC at deeper pairs.
155	The observed correlation is an artifact of 0v1 separation rather than fine-grained depth signal.
156	
```

```text
1	\section{Consistency Check: $\Kstar$-Guided Filtering}
2	\label{sec:filtering}
3	
4	$\Kempirical = 2$ makes a falsifiable prediction: binary curation should suffice, and graduated performance should converge toward binary as the decay steepness increases.
5	We test this as a consistency check on the boundary measurement, not as an independent contribution.
6	We compare six strategies on 30{,}000 documents (depths 0--5): (i)~\textbf{no-filter}, (ii)~\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\text{synthetic})$ from 15D LightGBM), (iii)~\textbf{binary-Drayson}~\citep{drayson2025}, (iv)~\textbf{graduated} ($w = \exp(-\alpha \cdot \hat{d})$, $\alpha \in \{0.1,\ldots,0.9\}$), (v)~\textbf{surprisal} (retain above median), and (vi)~\textbf{DSIR}~\citep{xie2023dsir}.
7	We fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\alpha_{\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs on MMLU and HellaSwag across 5 seeds (51 total runs; this 100\% synthetic setting is an upper bound~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}).
8	
9	\begin{table}[t]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\caption{Downstream task performance (MMLU, HellaSwag) under six data-filtering strategies. Binary-ours uses the 15D LightGBM depth classifier; Graduated applies exponentially decaying weights $w = \exp(-\alpha \hat{d})$. Mean $\pm$ std over 5 seeds. Full $\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}
13	\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}
14	\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}
15	\end{table}
16	
17	Binary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\,pp (${\sim}$50\% reduction; $p_{\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\ all alternatives).
18	These effects are measured in a 100\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower, and effect sizes would scale accordingly.
19	The strategy ordering is interpretable through $\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.
20	Graduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\Delta = 0$, $p_{\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} = 0.011$).
21	DSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter, confirming that depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).
22	At realistic contamination ratios ($\leq$50\%), all filtering strategies converge ($p > 0.12$), consistent with the one-wash laundering finding that depth-aware curation offers marginal benefit when $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response}).
23	For $\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations, depth-aware curation reduces to standard binary filtering, consistent with $\Kempirical{=}1$ implying no exploitable depth signal beyond the 0v1 boundary.
24	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.
5	$\Kempirical \in \{1, 2, 3\}$ depends on source domain and model characteristics: general web text yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ in 6/10 configurations (three of six families contain $\Kempirical{\geq}2$ configurations, with a scale-dependent split in Pythia; 1B--8B; $p \leq 0.002$), with $\Kempirical{=}3$ for structured academic text.
6	This one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.
7	For practitioners, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\ $\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal; graduated weighting beyond $\Kempirical$ adds noise.
8	$\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent (15D provides a conservative lower bound; neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures) and established for 1B--8B models; scaling beyond 8B, additional structured domains, and retrain-chain $\Kstar$ remain open.
9	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Consistency Check: $\\Kstar$-Guided Filtering}\n\\label{sec:filtering}\n\n$\\Kempirical = 2$ makes a falsifiable prediction: binary curation should suffice, and graduated performance should converge toward binary as the decay steepness increases.\nWe test this as a consistency check on the boundary measurement, not as an independent contribution.\nWe compare six strategies on 30{,}000 documents (depths 0--5): (i)~\\textbf{no-filter}, (ii)~\\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\\text{synthetic})$ from 15D LightGBM), (iii)~\\textbf{binary-Drayson}~\\citep{drayson2025}, (iv)~\\textbf{graduated} ($w = \\exp(-\\alpha \\cdot \\hat{d})$, $\\alpha \\in \\{0.1,\\ldots,0.9\\}$), (v)~\\textbf{surprisal} (retain above median), and (vi)~\\textbf{DSIR}~\\citep{xie2023dsir}.\nWe fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\\alpha_{\\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs on MMLU and HellaSwag across 5 seeds (51 total runs; this 100\\% synthetic setting is an upper bound~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Downstream task performance (MMLU, HellaSwag) under six data-filtering strategies. Binary-ours uses the 15D LightGBM depth classifier; Graduated applies exponentially decaying weights $w = \\exp(-\\alpha \\hat{d})$. Mean $\\pm$ std over 5 seeds. Full $\\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}\n\\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}\n\\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}\n\\end{table}\n\nBinary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\\,pp (${\\sim}$50\\% reduction; $p_{\\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\\ all alternatives).\nThese effects are measured in a 100\\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower, and effect sizes would scale accordingly.\nThe strategy ordering is interpretable through $\\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.\nGraduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\\Delta = 0$, $p_{\\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\nDSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter, confirming that depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).\nAt realistic contamination ratios ($\\leq$50\\%), all filtering strategies converge ($p > 0.12$), consistent with the one-wash laundering finding that depth-aware curation offers marginal benefit when $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\nFor $\\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations, depth-aware curation reduces to standard binary filtering, consistent with $\\Kempirical{=}1$ implying no exploitable depth signal beyond the 0v1 boundary.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013cWF9jumZHh9Y44qN6YxcH`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	\label{sec:why_kstar}
4	\label{sec:one_wash}
5	\label{sec:negative_graduated}
6	\label{sec:repr_dependence}
7	\label{sec:boundary_cases}
8	\label{sec:length_confound}
9	
10	\paragraph{One-wash laundering (web text).}
11	For general web text, $\Kempirical{=}2$ reflects concentrated information loss: 0v1 is near-trivially separable ($\auc \geq 0.92$), 1v2 drops by ${\sim}$30\,pp, and 2v3 falls below $\theta$.
12	A single generation pass renders text indistinguishable from multiply recycled content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.
13	Converging evidence supports a data-intrinsic interpretation: OBD achieves 96\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 8--38\% (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\ OBD; Table~\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).
14	
15	\paragraph{Graduated filtering failure.}
16	For web text, 2v3 $\auc < \theta$ across all model configurations, so depth assignments for $d \geq 2$ approximate random labels; graduated weighting injects noise, explaining convergence toward binary at high $\alpha$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).
17	DSIR fails differently: bigram hashing captures domain shift but not depth-correlated signatures.
18	
19	\paragraph{Representation dependence.}
20	$\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent: frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$, while Pythia yields $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 2$ and OLMo yields $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 1$, consistent with its 15D boundary (Table~\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}).
21	The 15D features trade one depth level of sensitivity for cross-model consistency (6/10 configurations $\Kempirical{=}2$), making them the preferred operational choice.
22	Feature rankings are scorer-independent ($\rho > 0.90$); $\Kempirical$ agreement is scorer-independent for strong-signal configurations (e.g., Qwen-rewrite retains $\Kempirical{=}2$ under all scorers) but scorer-sensitive at marginal boundaries (Qwen-continuation drops to $\Kempirical{=}1$; length confound ruled out in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).
23	ANOVA confirms this layered pattern: Scorer explains modest variance ($\eta^2_p{=}.166$) with a significant Generator$\times$Scorer interaction ($\eta^2_p{=}.146$; Table~\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Domain dependence of $\Kempirical$.}
26	Web text (C4, Wikipedia) consistently yields $\Kempirical{=}2$, while structured academic text (arXiv) yields $\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent model families (Qwen-1.5B, Pythia-1.4B; Section~\ref{sec:cross_domain}).
27	Domain-specific stylistic constraints amplify per-generation distributional shift, making contamination in structured corpora trackable to greater depth.
28	
29	\paragraph{Prompt-chain scope and retrain-chain gap.}
30	Our prompt-chain protocol targets the dominant web contamination scenario: LLM-generated text is published, scraped into training corpora, and recursively re-generated by downstream models without retraining on the outputs.
31	Retrain-chain experiments (Pythia-1.4B LoRA) yield $\Kempirical{\geq}4$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.9484$, 3v4 $= 0.7982$), far exceeding the prompt-chain boundary of $\Kempirical{=}2$, because each retrain generation also modifies model parameters, introducing cumulative adaptation artifacts (vocabulary narrowing, repetition bias) absent from text-only recycling.
32	Prompt-chain $\Kempirical$ therefore isolates text-intrinsic degradation, the operationally relevant quantity for data curation pipelines that filter documents without knowledge of the generator's training history.
33	Our conclusions do not directly transfer to retrain-chain (model collapse) settings, where the higher $\Kempirical$ reflects model-level rather than text-level information loss.
34	
35	\paragraph{Boundary cases and scale effect.}
36	$\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations cluster at 1v2 $\auc$ 0.508--0.598, well separated from the $\Kempirical{=}2$ range (0.611--0.696); $\Kempirical{=}1$ is not a classifier failure but a text property, as these models produce first-generation output so human-like that statistical features cannot distinguish depth~1 from depth~2.
37	That $\Kempirical \in \{1, 2\}$ for all web text configurations is the core empirical result; larger models reduce 0v1 JSD (0.077 at 8B vs.\ 0.15--0.27 at $\leq$1.5B), suggesting 13B+ may increasingly yield $\Kempirical{=}1$.
38	
39	\paragraph{Decoding strategy as confound.}
40	Greedy decoding inflates depth separability: Pythia-6.9B yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ under greedy but $\Kempirical{=}1$ under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$), with the borderline 1v2 score (2 of 5 folds above $\theta$) illustrating that the boundary is continuous rather than a cliff (Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_decoding}).
41	Practitioners should calibrate $\theta$ to their application-specific tolerance (Figure~\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).
42	
43	\paragraph{What $\Kempirical$ reveals.}
44	As a measurement tool, $\Kempirical$ provides explanatory power beyond a point estimate: it establishes why binary detection suffices for web text (depth${>}1$ is invisible to statistical features), identifies domains where finer strategies are viable (arXiv $\Kempirical{=}3$ vs.\ web $\Kempirical{=}2$), and quantifies how scale, scorer, and threshold jointly shape the boundary.
45	The OBD framework provides a reproducible, low-cost depth estimation protocol requiring only corpus-level statistical features, unlike detection methods that need API access or model weights.
46	Future work may explore scorer-ensemble strategies to improve cross-scorer robustness at marginal boundaries.
47	
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\n$\\Kempirical \\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.\nAmong the six nominal $\\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.\n\nThe practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\ $\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.\nThe consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\\,pp vs.\\ $-$1.53\\,pp for no-filter; Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\nFor structured domains where $\\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.\n\nThe 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\\% accuracy across model families, and feature rankings remain stable ($\\rho > 0.90$) under alternative scorers.\nThis scorer independence makes the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models, the common scenario in web-scale data curation where the generating model is rarely identified.\n$\\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, providing a principled basis for curation design that replaces ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.\n\nSeveral directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\n"
}
```

    "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\n$\\Kempirical \\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.\nAmong the six nominal $\\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.\n\nThe practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\ $\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.\nThe consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\\,pp vs.\\ $-$1.53\\,pp for no-filter; Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\nFor structured domains where $\\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.\n\nThe 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\\% accuracy across model families, and feature rankings remain stable ($\\rho > 0.90$) under alternative scorers.\nThis scorer independence makes the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models, the common scenario in web-scale data curation where the generating model is rarely identified.\n$\\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, providing a principled basis for curation design that replaces ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.\n\nSeveral directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\n",

  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "da0ffd9a-a118-4d69-9ddb-bcfb50fca730",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
    "oldString": "Extending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.",
    "newString": "Extending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\nAs recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\n$\\Kempirical \\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.\nAmong the six nominal $\\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.\n\nThe practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\ $\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.\nThe consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\\,pp vs.\\ $-$1.53\\,pp for no-filter; Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\nFor structured domains where $\\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.\n\nThe 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\\% accuracy across model families, and feature rankings remain stable ($\\rho > 0.90$) under alternative scorers.\nThis scorer independence makes the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models, the common scenario in web-scale data curation where the generating model is rarely identified.\n$\\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, providing a principled basis for curation design that replaces ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.\n\nSeveral directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\n",

          " $\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.",
          " Extending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.",
          "+As recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption."

  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "Several directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\nAs recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption.",
  "new_string": "Several directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nThe retrain-chain setting, where each generation also modifies model parameters, yields substantially higher $\\Kempirical$ ($\\geq$4 for Pythia-1.4B LoRA); bridging the prompt-chain and retrain-chain boundaries would connect our text-level measurement to the model collapse literature.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical text, where stylistic constraints may amplify depth signal as observed for arXiv), and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\nAs recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption."
}

    "oldString": "Several directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\nAs recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption.",
    "newString": "Several directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nThe retrain-chain setting, where each generation also modifies model parameters, yields substantially higher $\\Kempirical$ ($\\geq$4 for Pythia-1.4B LoRA); bridging the prompt-chain and retrain-chain boundaries would connect our text-level measurement to the model collapse literature.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical text, where stylistic constraints may amplify depth signal as observed for arXiv), and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\nAs recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\n$\\Kempirical \\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\\Kempirical \\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.\nAmong the six nominal $\\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.\n\nThe practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\ $\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.\nThe consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\\,pp vs.\\ $-$1.53\\,pp for no-filter; Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\nFor structured domains where $\\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.\n\nThe 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\\% accuracy across model families, and feature rankings remain stable ($\\rho > 0.90$) under alternative scorers.\nThis scorer independence makes the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models, the common scenario in web-scale data curation where the generating model is rarely identified.\n$\\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, providing a principled basis for curation design that replaces ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.\n\nSeveral directions remain open.\nAs model scale increases, the 0v1 signal attenuates (JSD $0.077$ at 8B vs.\\ $0.15$--$0.27$ at $\\leq$1.5B), suggesting that models beyond 13B may increasingly yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$, collapsing depth estimation to the binary boundary already dominant at current scales; tracking this trend is important for the continued viability of depth estimation.\n$\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.\nExtending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical), retrain-chain generation protocols, and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.\nAs recursive synthetic content continues to accumulate in web corpora, $\\Kempirical$ offers a quantitative basis for deciding how much depth resolution a curation pipeline can extract, anchoring filtering decisions in measurement rather than assumption.\n",

          " $\\Kempirical$ is feature-set dependent: the 15D features provide a conservative lower bound, while neural probes extend it by one level for some architectures but sacrifice cross-model consistency.",

          "+Extending the measurement to models beyond 8B, additional structured domains (legal, medical text, where stylistic constraints may amplify depth signal as observed for arXiv), and scorer-ensemble strategies for improved robustness at marginal boundaries are natural next steps.",

_source line 18448 · 2026-05-22T11:40:00.065Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] 基于 Reviewer 叙事评估，4 个小修改：

**1. Abstract + Conclusion 叙事顺序调整**
当前："K*≤3 across all ten configurations" 出现在 "K*≤2 for web text" 之前。应反过来——"K*≤2 web text" 是更锐利的 finding，放前面；"≤3 universally" 作为附带泛化放后面。Abstract 和 Conclusion 都检查调整。

**2. Discussion 加 Bonferroni 同步句**
Discussion "boundary cases" 或 "What K* reveals" 段当前没提 Bonferroni dual report，但 Abstract 和 Experiments 都提了。加一句类似："Under Bonferroni correction (99.5% CI for 10 simultaneous tests), four of the six nominal K*=2 configurations retain significance, while two boundary cases (Qwen-cont and LLaMA-8B at AUC ≈ 0.598) fall marginally below θ." 使三处叙事同步。

**3. Discussion "What K* reveals" 段补强实用价值 [重要]**
Reviewer 指出攻击面：reviewer 会问 "如果 realistic contamination 下 filtering 没区别，为什么需要 K*？"。在 "What K* reveals" 段加 2-3 句直接回应：
- K* 的价值不在改变 filtering 策略（binary 已足够），而在于：(1) 为 binary sufficiency 提供 empirical justification 而非 heuristic assumption；(2) 识别 domain-specific exceptions（arXiv K*=3 允许 graduated filtering）；(3) 作为 monitoring metric 追踪模型/数据演变趋势
- 写成自然段落融入 Discussion，不要写成编号列表

**4. Cross-model transfer 叙事微调**
Reviewer 指出 cross-model transfer (80-100% retention) 被 K* characterization 盖住了。在 Abstract 的 contribution (a) 或 Discussion 中，稍微突出 "model-agnostic depth estimation without retraining" 的实用意义。不需要大改，加半句即可。

写作规则：无破折号、无 AI 风格。编译后确认 8 页主体不溢出。

23	
24	Our contributions:
25	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
26	  \item \textbf{$\Kempirical$ as a measurement tool} across 10 model configurations (6 families, 1B--8B) and 4 decoding strategies ($p \leq 0.002$; Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}):
27	  $\Kempirical \leq 3$ universally; for web text, $\Kempirical \leq 2$ regardless of family or scale.
28	  $\Kempirical$ establishes binary sufficiency for web text by measurement (not assumption), reveals domain-dependent variation (arXiv $\Kempirical{=}3$), and quantifies boundary sensitivity to threshold, scorer, and model scale.
29	  Both supervised OBD and zero-shot LLMs fail beyond $\Kempirical$ (McNemar $p > 0.05$; Section~\ref{sec:why_kstar}).
30	  \item \textbf{Domain dependence of $\Kempirical$}: general web text (C4, Wikipedia) yields $\Kempirical{=}2$, while structured academic text (arXiv) yields $\Kempirical{=}3$, consistent across model families (Section~\ref{sec:cross_domain}).
31	  \item \textbf{Model-free cross-model transfer}: 15 statistical features enable 80--100\% accuracy retention with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$); $\Kempirical$ agreement holds across scorers for strong-signal configurations (Section~\ref{sec:transfer}).
32	\end{enumerate}
33	
34	\noindent As a consistency check, $\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts the strategy ordering for web-text filtering: binary curation minimizes degradation most effectively (MMLU $0.5941$ vs.\ base $0.6018$; Section~\ref{sec:filtering}).

_source line 18453 · 2026-05-22T11:40:16.342Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013ia4JokarAGZdPpLqKHaZw`</summary>

```text
32	
33	% === Abstract ===
34	\begin{abstract}
35	Web-scale corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.
36	We measure a lower bound on the generational discrimination boundary, $\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain distinguishable), using 15 statistical features and an OBD-LightGBM classifier.
37	Under prompt-chain generation across 10 model configurations (6 architecture families, 1B--8B), 3 source domains, and 4 decoding strategies, $\Kempirical \leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six families, 1B--8B) at $\theta{=}0.60$; for web text (C4, Wikipedia), $\Kempirical \leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, with structured academic text (arXiv) reaching $\Kempirical{=}3$ across two architectures.
38	Among the six nominal $\Kempirical{=}2$ configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.
39	A single generation pass suffices to render general web text indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content, a \emph{one-wash laundering} effect.
40	Model-free statistical features enable 80--100\% cross-model transfer with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$).
41	As a consistency check, domain-appropriate $\Kempirical$ correctly predicts that binary filtering outperforms graduated alternatives for web text when contamination causes detectable quality loss.
```

</details>

_source line 18455 · 2026-05-22T11:40:16.877Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QkTmX7u3PdYc85oEqym6qd`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.
5	$\Kempirical \leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\Kempirical \leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.
6	Among the six nominal $\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.
7	This one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.
8	
9	The practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\ $\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.
10	The consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\,pp vs.\ $-$1.53\,pp for no-filter; Section~\ref{sec:filtering}).
11	For structured domains where $\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.
12	
13	The 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\% accuracy across model families, and scorer-independent feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$) make the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models.
14	$\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, replacing ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.
15	Several directions remain open: tracking $\Kempirical$ as model scale surpasses 13B (where 0v1 JSD attenuation suggests $\Kempirical{=}1$ may become dominant), bridging the prompt-chain and retrain-chain boundaries to connect text-level measurement with the model collapse literature, and extending to additional structured domains and scorer-ensemble strategies.
16	
```
